Claude Opus~4.6 shows more uniform errors across depths 1--4,\n    with depth-4 recall (0.3700) substantially exceeding GPT-5.5.\n    Per-depth recall comparison with OBD-LightGBM in Table~\\ref{tab:s3c_perdepth}.}\n  \\label{tab:s3_llm_zeroshot}\n\n  \\vspace{0.5em}\n  \\begin{subtable}{0.48\\textwidth}\n    \\centering\n    \\caption{GPT-5.5 ($N{=}500$, acc${=}0.332$, MAE${=}1.102$)}\n    \\label{tab:s3a_gpt55}\n    \\small\n    \\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n      \\toprule\n      & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{Predicted Depth} & \\\\\n      \\cmidrule(lr){2-6}\n      True & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & Rec. \\\\\n      \\midrule\n      0 & \\textbf{77} & 18 &  4 &  0 &  1 & .770 \\\\\n      1 &  9 & \\textbf{19} & 36 & 24 & 12 & .190 \\\\\n      2 &  8 & 10 & \\textbf{38} & 35 &  9 & .380 \\\\\n      3 & 10 & 16 & 35 & \\textbf{24} & 15 & .240 \\\\\n      4 & 11 & 13 & 50 & 18 &  \\textbf{8} & .080 \\\\\n      \\bottomrule\n    \\end{tabular}\n  \\end{subtable}\n  \\hfill\n  \\begin{subtable}{0.48\\textwidth}\n    \\centering\n    \\caption{Claude Opus~4.6 ($N{=}499$, acc${=}0.331$, MAE${=}1.150$)}\n    \\label{tab:s3b_claude}\n    \\small\n    \\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n      \\toprule\n      & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{Predicted Depth} & \\\\\n      \\cmidrule(lr){2-6}\n      True & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & Rec. \\\\\n      \\midrule\n      0 & \\textbf{69} & 26 &  3 &  0 &  2 & .690 \\\\\n      1 &  8 & \\textbf{21} & 32 & 11 & 28 & .210 \\\\\n      2 &  6 & 36 & \\textbf{28} &  5 & 25 & .280 \\\\\n      3 &  7 & 20 & 31 & \\textbf{10} & 31 & .100 \\\\\n      4 &  7 & 28 & 22 &  6 & \\textbf{37} & .370 \\\\\n      \\bottomrule\n    \\end{tabular}\n  \\end{subtable}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\small\n  \\caption{Per-depth recall (\\%) on balanced $N{=}500$ test set. OBD's nominal overall accuracy gap originates from depth-0; all methods fail at depths $\\geq 2$, consistent with $\\Kempirical = 2$.}\n  \\label{tab:s3c_perdepth}\n  \\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n  \\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n    \\toprule\n    & Depth 0 & Depth 1 & Depth 2 & Depth 3 & Depth 4 \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    OBD-LightGBM & \\textbf{96} & \\textbf{39} & 14 & 19 & 22 \\\\\n    GPT-5.5      & 77 & 19 & \\textbf{38} & \\textbf{24} &  8 \\\\\n    Claude Opus~4.6 & 69 & 21 & 28 & 10 & \\textbf{37} \\\\\n    \\bottomrule\n  \\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Zero-shot prompt template.}\nBoth GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus~4.6 received the following system prompt (temperature 0, single-token output constraint):\n\n\\begin{quote}\n\\small\n\\texttt{The following text passage was produced through recursive LLM generation starting from human-written text (depth~0). Each successive generation feeds the previous output back to the LLM: depth~0 = human, depth~1 = first LLM pass, depth~2 = second pass, etc.}\n\n\\texttt{Estimate the generational depth (0, 1, 2, 3, or 4) of the passage below. Respond with only the depth number.}\n\n\\texttt{Text: \\{text\\}}\n\\end{quote}\n\n%%% Table: Token Length and EOS Truncation Analysis\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\small\n  \\caption{Mean subword token count per depth and EOS truncation rate (\\%) for depth~1+ across all model configurations (generation limit: 256 tokens). Depth~0 (human text) is shared across configurations. Depths 1--5 show no systematic length trend within any model; the 0v1 length asymmetry (human texts shorter) makes detection \\emph{more conservative}, not inflated. See Section~\\ref{sec:length_confound} for discussion.}\n  \\label{tab:token_length}\n  \\begin{tabular}{lcccccccc}\n    \\toprule\n    & \\multicolumn{6}{c}{\\textbf{Mean Token Count}} & & \\\\\n    \\cmidrule(lr){2-7}\n    \\textbf{Model} & Depth 0 & Depth 1 & Depth 2 & Depth 3 & Depth 4 & Depth 5 & \\textbf{EOS Trunc.\\ (\\%)} & \\textbf{Severity} \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    Qwen-rw (1.5B)  & 191 & 254 & 252 & 254 & 254 & 254 & 7--11 & Mild \\\\\n    Pythia-1.4B      & 192 & 214 & 215 & 216 & 217 & 218 & 34--36 & High \\\\\n    OLMo-1B          & 192 & 256 & 256 & 256 & 256 & 256 & $<$1 & Negligible \\\\\n    Qwen-cont (1.5B) & 191 & 244 & 238 & 240 & 240 & 238 & 17--20 & Moderate \\\\\n    Qwen-7B          & 191 & 196 & 195 & 193 & 197 & 197 & 45--47 & High \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    \\multicolumn{9}{l}{\\textit{Decoding ablation (Qwen-rw 1.5B)}} \\\\\n    \\quad Greedy          & 191 & 253 & 252 & 253 & 254 & 254 & 8--12 & Mild \\\\\n    \\quad Nuc $p{=}0.9$   & 191 & 254 & 251 & 254 & 253 & 254 & 8--16 & Mild \\\\\n    \\quad $T{=}0.7$       & 191 & 253 & 251 & 253 & 253 & 254 & 8--14 & Mild \\\\\n    \\quad $T{=}1.2$       & 191 & 254 & 252 & 253 & 254 & 254 & 8--16 & Mild \\\\\n    \\bottomrule\n  \\end{tabular}\n\n  \\vspace{4pt}\n  \\footnotesize\n  EOS truncation rate: percentage of depth~1+ samples where generation terminated via EOS before reaching the 256-token limit. Ranges span depths 1--5.\n\\end{table*}\n\n%%% Table S4: Dataset Statistics\n\\begin{table}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\small\n  \\caption{Dataset statistics for the generational depth corpus. Depth~0 contains human-written seed texts sampled from the C4 English validation split and truncated to 256 subword tokens. Depths~1--5 are recursively generated by Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (nucleus sampling, $p{=}0.95$, $T{=}1.0$). Token counts use whitespace tokenization. Statistics shown for the primary Qwen-rewrite corpus; the other three model-mode configurations (Pythia-cont, OLMo-cont, Qwen-cont) share the same generation pipeline and corpus structure.}\n  \\label{tab:s4_dataset_stats}\n  \\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n  \\begin{tabular}{clrl}\n    \\toprule\n    \\textbf{Depth} & \\textbf{$N$ samples} & \\textbf{Mean tokens} & \\textbf{Source} \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    0 & 5{,}000 & 147.8 & C4 validation (human) \\\\\n    1 & 5{,}000 & 207.1 & Qwen2.5-1.5B rewrite \\\\\n    2 & 5{,}000 & 206.1 & Qwen2.5-1.5B rewrite \\\\\n    3 & 5{,}000 & 208.0 & Qwen2.5-1.5B rewrite \\\\\n    4 & 5{,}000 & 207.7 & Qwen2.5-1.5B rewrite \\\\\n    5 & 5{,}000 & 207.7 & Qwen2.5-1.5B rewrite \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    \\multicolumn{2}{l}{Total} & \\multicolumn{2}{l}{30{,}000 samples} \\\\\n    \\bottomrule\n  \\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n%%% Table S5: Complete 15-D Feature Descriptions\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\caption{Complete description of the 15-dimensional feature set used by the OBD classifier. Features are grouped into three families: surprisal statistics (9~dims) computed from Qwen2.5-1.5B token-level log-probabilities, lexical diversity (3~dims), and frequency tail shape (3~dims). All features are computed per document.}\n  \\label{tab:s5_features}\n  \\begin{tabular}{llp{0.45\\textwidth}l}\n    \\toprule\n    \\textbf{Group} & \\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{Description} & \\textbf{Range} \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    \\multirow{9}{*}{\\rotatebox[origin=c]{90}{\\textsc{Surprisal}}}\n    & \\texttt{surp\\_mean}   & Mean token surprisal $\\frac{1}{T}\\sum_t -\\log p(x_t \\mid x_{&lt;t})$ & $\\mathbb{R}^+$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{surp\\_std}    & Standard deviation of token surprisal & $\\mathbb{R}^+$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{surp\\_skew}   & Skewness of the surprisal distribution & $\\mathbb{R}$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{surp\\_kurt}   & Excess kurtosis of the surprisal distribution & $\\mathbb{R}$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_mean} & Mean of first-order surprisal differences $\\Delta s_t = s_{t+1} - s_t$ & $\\mathbb{R}$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}  & Std of first-order surprisal differences & $\\mathbb{R}^+$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_skew} & Skewness of first-order surprisal differences & $\\mathbb{R}$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_mean} & Mean of second-order differences $\\Delta^2 s_t$ & $\\mathbb{R}$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}  & Std of second-order surprisal differences & $\\mathbb{R}^+$ \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    \\multirow{3}{*}{\\rotatebox[origin=c]{90}{\\textsc{Lex}}}\n    & \\texttt{ttr}          & Type-token ratio: $|V| / T$ & $[0, 1]$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{hapax\\_ratio} & Hapax legomena ratio: freq-1 types / unique types & $[0, 1]$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{self\\_bleu}   & Mean 4-gram BLEU against 100 random same-depth samples & $[0, 1]$ \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    \\multirow{3}{*}{\\rotatebox[origin=c]{90}{\\textsc{Freq}}}\n    & \\texttt{freq\\_kurtosis}  & Excess kurtosis of word frequency distribution & $\\mathbb{R}$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{freq\\_entropy}   & Shannon entropy of word frequencies: $-\\sum p_w \\log_2 p_w$ & $\\mathbb{R}^+$ \\\\\n    & \\texttt{low\\_freq\\_ratio} & Proportion of vocabulary with count $\\leq 2$ & $[0, 1]$ \\\\\n    \\bottomrule\n  \\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n%%% Tables S6 & S7: Side by side in a single table* to avoid table*/table overlap\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.45\\textwidth}\n  \\centering\n  \\small\n  \\captionof{table}{LightGBM hyperparameters for the pairwise OBD (Ordinal Binary Decomposition) classifier used throughout the paper. One binary classifier is trained per adjacent depth pair $(k, k{+}1)$; 5-fold GroupKFold cross-validation is grouped by \\texttt{doc\\_id} to prevent data leakage from the same seed document appearing in both train and test.}\n  \\label{tab:s6_lgbm_params}\n  \\begin{tabular}{ll}\n    \\toprule\n    \\textbf{Parameter} & \\textbf{Value} \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    \\texttt{n\\_estimators}      & 200 \\\\\n    \\texttt{learning\\_rate}     & 0.05 \\\\\n    \\texttt{max\\_depth}         & 6 \\\\\n    \\texttt{num\\_leaves}        & 31 \\\\\n    \\texttt{verbose}            & $-1$ \\\\\n    \\texttt{n\\_jobs}            & $-1$ (all cores) \\\\\n    Cross-validation            & 5-fold GroupKFold \\\\\n    Grouping key                & \\texttt{doc\\_id} \\\\\n    Objective                   & Binary (per depth pair) \\\\\n    \\bottomrule\n  \\end{tabular}\n\\end{minipage}\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.52\\textwidth}\n  \\centering\n  \\small\n  \\captionof{table}{Neural probe architecture and training details (Exp-015). Applied to all three model families (Qwen2.5-1.5B, Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B): each model's frozen hidden states are average-pooled into document embeddings and classified by a two-hidden-layer MLP.}\n  \\label{tab:s7_neural_probe}\n  \\resizebox{\\textwidth}{!}{%\n  \\begin{tabular}{ll}\n    \\toprule\n    \\textbf{Component} & \\textbf{Details} \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    \\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Embedding extraction}} \\\\\n    Base models       & Qwen2.5-1.5B / Pythia-1.4B / OLMo-1B (frozen) \\\\\n    Representation    & Last hidden state, attention-masked avg pool \\\\\n    Embedding dim     & 1536 / 2048 / 2048 \\\\\n    Max sequence len   & 512 tokens \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    \\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{MLP classifier}} \\\\\n    Architecture      & $d_{\\text{emb}} \\to 512 \\to 256 \\to 6$ \\\\\n    Activations       & ReLU \\\\\n    Dropout           & 0.1 (after each hidden layer) \\\\\n    Output            & 6-class softmax \\\\\n    \\midrule\n    \\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textit{Training}} \\\\\n    Optimizer         & Adam ($\\text{lr} = 10^{-3}$, weight decay $= 10^{-4}$) \\\\\n    Loss              & Cross-entropy \\\\\n    Batch size        & 256 \\\\\n    Max epochs        & 20 \\\\\n    Early stopping    & Patience = 5 (validation loss) \\\\\n    Cross-validation  & 5-fold GroupKFold \\\\\n    \\bottomrule\n  \\end{tabular}%\n  }\n\\end{minipage}\n\\end{table*}\n\n%%% Table S9: Binary Detector Spearman ρ (exp-012)\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\small\n  \\caption{Spearman rank correlation ($\\rho$) between binary detector confidence scores and true generational depth, and 6-class depth classification accuracy (Exp-012). Three metrics: log-PPL from the reference model (DivEye), log-PPL from the observer model, and PPL ratio (Binoculars; observer: Pythia-1.4B, performer: OLMo-1B). 33	\subsection{Extended Limitations}
34	\label{sec:extended_limitations}
35	
36	\emph{Prompt-chain versus retrain-chain.}
37	Both Qwen2.5-1.5B and Pythia-1.4B yield $\Kempirical{=}7$ under retrain-chain LoRA fine-tuning (Table~\ref{tab:retrain_ci}), extending detectable depth by 5 additional generations beyond prompt-chain $\Kempirical{=}2$.
38	Cumulative model-adaptation artifacts (vocabulary narrowing, repetition bias) make each retrained generation highly distinguishable.
39	The two models exhibit different decay profiles: Pythia achieves higher early-pair AUC (1v2: 0.947 vs.\ 0.897) but drops more steeply at deeper pairs (4v5: 0.691 vs.\ 0.746), yet both converge to $\Kempirical{=}7$ (6v7: Qwen 0.641, Pythia 0.634; both CI lower bounds $> \theta$).
40	This convergence despite differences in effective LoRA adaptation capacity (Pythia uses fused QKV attention with rank-16 vs.\ Qwen's separate Q/K/V projections) indicates robustness to LoRA parameterization.
41	The extended discriminability under retraining aligns with theoretical analyses of iterative retraining instability on synthetic data~\cite{bertrand2024stability, gerstgrasser2024model}, where each retraining cycle amplifies distributional artifacts beyond the text-intrinsic degradation observed in prompt-chains.
42	Extrapolating yields 7v8 $\approx 0.57 < \theta$ for both models. This places $\Kempirical{=}7$ near the ceiling for retrain-chains.
43	Prompt-chain $\Kempirical$ represents the operationally relevant boundary for data curation (text-intrinsic information loss); retrain-chain $\Kempirical$ represents the upper bound achievable via model archaeology (known training pipeline).

67	\paragraph{CI methodology note.}
68	\label{sec:retrain_ci_note}
69	The main pipeline reports bootstrap 95\% CIs (1{,}000 or 10{,}000 resamples), which are valid because each bootstrap sample preserves the GroupKFold document structure: resampling operates on documents, and all depths of a given document remain together.
70	In the retrain-chain setting, each generation's training corpus is the \emph{output} of the previous generation's fine-tuned model. This creates inter-generation dependencies that standard bootstrap resampling cannot preserve, and naive resampling would inflate AUC estimates.
71	We therefore report fold-based 95\% CIs for retrain-chain experiments, computed as $\bar{x} \pm 1.96 \cdot s / \sqrt{K}$ from the $K{=}5$ GroupKFold AUC values.
72	
73	\emph{Threshold sensitivity.}
74	Table~\ref{tab:theta_sensitivity} reports $\Kempirical$ across $\theta \in [0.55, 0.70]$ for representative configurations including arXiv and cross-scorer conditions.
75	Qwen-continuation's 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\theta$ by only 0.2\,pp; all ten boundary comparisons yield permutation $p < 0.001$.

109	
110	\emph{Structural confound: token length.}
111	In continuation mode the generation prompt is discarded, so all depths $\geq 1$ consist entirely of machine-generated tokens and the 1v2 boundary compares texts differing only in conditioning context (human-authored vs.\ machine-generated prompt), not structural composition.
112	Depth~0 texts average ${\sim}$191 subword tokens (C4 truncation), while depth~1+ texts average 193--256 tokens depending on model (Table~\ref{tab:token_length}).
113	This asymmetry is directionally opposite to a length-inflation confound: shorter depth-0 texts provide less feature-extraction context, making 0v1 discrimination harder, not easier.
114	Within depths 1--5, mean token counts are stable (within-model range $\leq$ 4 tokens for all configurations except Qwen-continuation at 6 tokens), so the pairwise comparisons underlying $\Kempirical{=}2$ are unaffected by length.
115	EOS truncation rates are similarly depth-stable within each model: Qwen-rewrite 7--11\%, Pythia 34--36\%, OLMo $<$1\%, Qwen-continuation 17--20\%, Qwen-7B 45--47\%.
116	Models with the highest truncation (Pythia, Qwen-7B) still yield $\Kempirical{=}2$ with the same step-function AUC decay pattern as low-truncation models (Table~\ref{tab:kstar_ci}), EOS truncation therefore does not drive the boundary.
117	
118	\emph{Graduated filtering.}
119	All $\alpha$ values (0.1--0.9) underperform binary-ours on MMLU ($\alpha{=}0.1$: 0.5873; $\alpha{=}0.9$: 0.5892 vs.\ 0.5941); at $\alpha{=}0.9$, HellaSwag matches exactly (0.6701).
120	Binary-ours significantly outperforms all alternatives on MMLU ($p_{\text{corr}} \leq 0.021$); Graduated$_{0.9}$ is indistinguishable on HellaSwag ($p = 1.00$) but worse on MMLU ($p_{\text{corr}} = 0.011$).
121	\label{tab:pairwise_significance}

124	\label{sec:dose_response}
125

148	At all tested ratios (10--50\%), no-filter MMLU remains above the base model (0.6018), synthetic data at these proportions is therefore net-beneficial rather than harmful.
149	Binary filtering consistently improves over no-filter, with HellaSwag differences significant across all ratios ($p \leq 0.004$, Holm-Bonferroni corrected).
150	MMLU differences are smaller and significant only at 10\% after correction ($p = 0.001$).
151	Graduated weighting is indistinguishable from no-filter at all ratios (MMLU $p \geq 0.27$; HellaSwag $p \geq 0.06$), depth assignments beyond $\Kempirical$ therefore inject noise regardless of contamination severity.
152	Effect sizes diminish 3--6$\times$ relative to the 100\% setting (Section~\ref{sec:filtering}), but the strategy ordering (binary $>$ graduated $\approx$ no-filter) is preserved.

226	\end{table}
227	
228	All four configurations preserve their $\Kempirical$ under 14D, with negligible AUC change ($|\Delta| \leq 0.003$).
229	The self\_BLEU feature contributes minimally to the depth boundary; $\Kempirical{=}2$ is driven by surprisal and entropy features rather than lexical repetition statistics.
230	
231	\subsection{Wikipedia Domain: Scorer Confound Resolution}
232	\label{sec:wiki_domain}
233	
234	Under same-family scoring (Qwen-1.5B scorer), Wikipedia $\Kempirical$ appeared to exhibit model-family dependence: Qwen generators (1.5B and 7B) yielded $\Kempirical{=}2$, while all four non-Qwen models yielded $\Kempirical{=}1$ (Table~\ref{tab:wiki_domain}).
235	A scorer confound ablation resolves this apparent split.
236	Rescoring the same Qwen-7B Wikipedia text with Pythia-1.4B reduces 1v2 $\auc$ from $0.611$ to $0.587$ ($\Delta = -2.4$\,pp), dropping $\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1.
237	With the scorer confound controlled, all six models yield $\Kempirical{=}1$ on Wikipedia, and the domain ordering simplifies to: arXiv ($\Kempirical{=}3$) $>$ C4 ($\Kempirical{=}2$) $>$ Wikipedia ($\Kempirical{=}1$).

265	The aggregate mean falls below $\theta$, confirming $\Kempirical{=}1$.

289	\subsection{JSD Decay Analysis}
290	\label{sec:jsd_extended}
291	
292	The token-level JSD between adjacent generations reveals two distinct decay regimes (Figure~\ref{fig:jsd_decay}).
293	For C4 web text, 0v1 JSD exceeds 1v2 by 6--56$\times$ across all 8 models (1B--14B) with $\Kempirical{=}2$ (median cliff ratio ${\sim}$15$\times$), and subsequent pairs converge to a noise floor of ${\sim}$0.003--0.005.
294	Fitting $\jsd(k, k{+}1) \propto e^{-\lambda k}$ yields $\lambda \approx 2.2$--$3.5$ for C4 (mean ${\approx}\,2.9$), consistent with rapid mixing of $G_\delta$ toward its stationary distribution.
295	These decay rates represent qualitative characterizations from 5-point curves rather than precise estimates.
296	The empirical decay rate $\lambda$ is consistent within each domain but varies across domains: C4 yields $\lambda \approx 2.9$, Wikipedia $\lambda \approx 3.1$ (excluding OLMo, whose $\lambda = 6.3$ reflects a capacity-limited collapse: 0v1 $\jsd = 0.169$ with near-complete information loss at the first step, the exponential fit is therefore unreliable), and arXiv $\lambda \approx 1.0$.
297	This cross-model consistency indicates that the contraction rate of $G_\delta$ is governed primarily by the structure of the input domain rather than the model architecture.
298	ArXiv text decays ${\sim}$3$\times$ slower than C4 ($\lambda \approx 1.0$ vs.\ $2.9$), with a correspondingly higher convergence floor (${\sim}$0.007--0.009 vs.\ ${\sim}$0.003--0.004).
299	Formulaic structural features (LaTeX formatting, citation patterns, section templates) degrade more slowly under autoregressive generation and supply persistent depth-distinguishing signal, this accounts for the elevated $\Kempirical{=}3$.
300	The retrain-chain exhibits a fundamentally different profile: $\lambda = 0.285$ with a cliff ratio of only 1.31$\times$ and no sharp drop between adjacent pairs (Figure~\ref{fig:retrain_decay}).
301	This gradual, sustained divergence reflects cumulative model-adaptation artifacts rather than text-intrinsic information loss, further supporting the distinction between prompt-chain and retrain-chain $\Kempirical$ (Appendix~\ref{sec:boundary_extended}).
302	
303	\begin{figure}[h]
304	\centering
305	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_retrain_decay.png}
306	\caption{Retrain-chain AUC decay across 7 generation pairs (Qwen2.5-1.5B LoRA). Unlike prompt-chain (sharp cliff at 0v1$\to$1v2), retrain-chain AUC decays gradually from 0.984 to 0.641, with all pairs above $\theta{=}0.60$. Error bars: 95\% CI from 5-fold CV. Dashed line: $\theta{=}0.60$; extrapolated 7v8 $\approx 0.57$ (below $\theta$), suggesting $\Kempirical{=}7$ is near the ceiling.}
307	\label{fig:retrain_decay}
308	\end{figure}
309	
310	\subsection{Boundary Cases and Scale Effect}
311	\label{sec:boundary_extended}
312	
313	Of 9 tested model configurations on C4, 8 yield $\Kempirical{=}2$; Mistral-7B is the only sub-2 exception ($\Kempirical{=}1$; 1v2 $\auc = 0.594$, CI $[0.584, 0.603]$), validating $\theta{=}0.60$ as an effective discriminator (gap: $0.594$ vs.\ $0.611$--$0.696$).
314	Mistral's cross-domain profile reinforces the domain-governed interpretation: despite $\Kempirical{=}1$ on C4 and Wikipedia, it yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ on code and $\Kempirical{=}3$ on arXiv (1v2 $\auc = 0.820$, 2v3 $= 0.628$), and this profile persists under cross-scorer validation (Pythia-1.4B scorer: C4 $\Kempirical{=}1$, arXiv $\Kempirical{=}3$ (2v3 $\auc = 0.608$, CI $[0.593, 0.622]$)), domain lexical diversity can therefore rescue even web-text one-wash generators (Table~\ref{tab:domain_kstar}).
315	Larger models produce more human-like text, reducing 0v1 JSD: 0.16--0.29 at $\leq$1.5B, 0.077--0.13 at 7--14B.
316	However, Qwen-14B still yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.621$, CI $[0.612, 0.630]$), with 0v1 $\auc = 0.960$ comparable to Qwen-7B ($0.964$), scale in the 1B--14B range therefore does not shift the discrimination boundary.
317	
318	\paragraph{Sample-size control.}
319	As a sample-size control, subsampling C4 to $n{=}2{,}200$ (matching arXiv) yields $\Kempirical{=}1$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.601$, CI $[0.586, 0.616]$).
320	Reduced sample size widens confidence intervals and produces more conservative $\Kempirical$ estimates, arXiv's $\Kempirical{=}3$ at the same sample size therefore reflects genuine domain signal rather than a statistical artifact of smaller training sets.
321

350	\subsection{Statistical Power Analysis}
351	\label{sec:power_curve}
352	
353	To characterize the minimum sample size required for reliable $\Kempirical$ determination, we subsample C4 at $n \in \{500, 1000, 2000, 3000, 4000, 5000\}$ and measure 1v2 AUC stability across 10 resamples per size (Qwen-1.5B-base continuation, 5-fold GroupKFold; Table~\ref{tab:power_curve}, Figure~\ref{fig:power_curve}).
354	
355	\begin{table}[h]
356	\centering
357	\small
358	\caption{Subsampling power analysis for $\Kempirical{=}2$ determination (C4, Qwen-1.5B-base, 10 resamples per $n$). Detection probability: fraction of resamples where 1v2 $\auc > \theta{=}0.60$.}
359	\label{tab:power_curve}
360	\begin{tabular}{rccc}
361	\toprule
362	$n$ & \textbf{Mean 1v2 $\auc$} & \textbf{Std} & \textbf{Detection Prob.} \\
363	\midrule

Detection rate reaches 100\% at $n{=}2{,}000$; the 80\% power threshold (dotted purple) is crossed between $n{=}1{,}000$ and $n{=}2{,}000$. Error bars: $\pm$1 std over 10 resamples. The dashed red line marks $\theta{=}0.60$.}
378	\label{fig:power_curve}
379	\end{figure}
380	
381	Detection rate jumps from 20\% ($n{=}500$) to 100\% ($n{=}2{,}000$), with the 80\% power threshold crossed near $n{=}1{,}500$.
382	At $n{=}5{,}000$ (the sample size used in all main experiments), variance is zero across resamples, our results are therefore well above the minimum required for reliable boundary estimation.
383	The sharp transition reflects the proximity of the 1v2 AUC to $\theta$: small increases in $n$ reduce variance sufficiently to move all resamples above the threshold.
384

424	\subsection{Classifier Choice: McNemar Test}
425	\label{sec:mcnemar_baselines}
426	
427	To verify that depth discriminability is driven by the 15D feature representation rather than classifier choice, we compare OBD-LightGBM against three baseline classifiers (logistic regression, linear SVC, random forest) on the depth-1 vs.\ depth-2 binary classification task using McNemar's test with continuity correction on 5-fold GroupKFold out-of-fold predictions (Table~\ref{tab:mcnemar_obd_baselines}).
428	Of 36 comparisons, 31 are non-significant ($p \geq 0.05$), and all five significant results concentrate in two model $\times$ domain conditions.
429	For LLaMA-8B on C4, all three baselines significantly outperform OBD ($p \leq 0.001$), likely reflecting LightGBM overfitting on the relatively noisy 8B-generated features rather than a systematic classifier advantage.
430	For Qwen-1.5B on Wikipedia, OBD outperforms both linear classifiers ($p < 0.001$) but not random forest ($p = 0.655$).

466	\subsection{Domain Lexical Statistics}
467	\label{sec:domain_lexical_stats}
468	
469	Table~\ref{tab:domain-ttr-entropy} reports type-token ratio (TTR) and vocabulary entropy across three domains and six depth levels.
470	The TTR ordering at depth 0 (arXiv $>$ C4 $>$ Wikipedia) aligns with the $\Kempirical$ ordering, with large effect sizes (Cohen's $d = 1.52$ for arXiv vs.\ Wikipedia).
471	Vocabulary entropy shows a partial reversal at depth 0: C4 ($H = 6.225$) falls below Wikipedia ($H = 6.408$), while arXiv remains highest ($H = 6.853$).
472	This reversal likely reflects C4's greater content heterogeneity (higher variance: $\sigma = 0.706$ vs.\ $0.284$), which inflates TTR but depresses mean entropy.
473	As depth increases, all domains converge, with arXiv exhibiting the steepest decline, consistent with its higher $\Kempirical$ and slower convergence to the stationary distribution.

505	\subsection{Leave-One-Family-Out Threshold Validation}
506	\label{sec:theta_lofo}
507	
508	To validate $\theta{=}0.60$ without circular reasoning, we perform leave-one-family-out (LOFO) cross-validation: each fold removes one architecture family, derives $\hat{\theta}$ as the midpoint between $\min(\auc_{1v2})$ and $\max(\auc_{2v3})$ over the remaining families, then predicts $\Kempirical$ for the held-out models.
509	Table~\ref{tab:theta_lofo} reports results across all three domains.
510	
511	On C4, 8/9 held-out predictions agree with the original $\Kempirical$ assignment at $\theta{=}0.60$ (agreement: 89\%).
512	The single mismatch is Qwen-1.5B-RW (rewrite mode), whose 2v3 AUC of 0.5893 lies above the LOFO-derived $\hat{\theta}{=}0.5725$ but below the global $\theta{=}0.60$; this configuration is also the only rewrite-mode model, the discrepancy is therefore attributable to mode rather than threshold instability.
513	The derived $\hat{\theta}$ values span $[0.5725, 0.6004]$, and $\theta{=}0.60$ falls within this range.
514	
515	ArXiv corroborates with 6/6 agreement (100\%), though the wide $\hat{\theta}$ range $[0.5735, 0.7296]$ reflects OLMo-1B's near-chance performance ($\auc_{1v2}{=}0.503$), which inflates the gap midpoint when OLMo is included in the training fold.
516	
517	Wikipedia yields only 1/6 agreement (17\%).
518	This failure is expected: most models produce near-chance 1v2 AUC on Wikipedia ($0.499$--$0.619$), so the gap between $\min(\auc_{1v2})$ and $\max(\auc_{2v3})$ is narrow or inverted, the gap-based threshold derivation is therefore invalid.
519	Wikipedia's $\Kempirical{=}1$ assignment is independently confirmed by the global $\theta{=}0.60$ criterion (Section~\ref{sec:cross_domain}); LOFO simply cannot validate this because the method requires a clear AUC discontinuity between boundary and non-boundary pairs.

610	\paragraph{Cross-scorer validation (Code domain).}
611	To rule out scorer confound for Code $\Kempirical{=}2$, we re-evaluate Qwen2.5-1.5B code generations using Pythia-1.4B as the reference scorer (Table~\ref{tab:code_crossscorer}).
612	Cross-family scoring yields $\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\auc = 0.7197$, five-fold minimum $= 0.6985$, all folds above $\theta$), a decrease of only 1.9\,pp from Qwen self-scoring ($0.7382$).
613	This contrasts with Wikipedia, where same-family scoring inflates $\Kempirical$ from 1 to 2 (Appendix~\ref{sec:wiki_domain}): Code depth signal is robust to scorer choice, while Wikipedia's marginal signal is scorer-sensitive.
614	The scorer confound is therefore domain-specific rather than systematic.

634	\subsection{Borderline Feature Analysis}
635	\label{sec:borderline_analysis}
636	
637	To understand why Mistral-7B yields $\Kempirical{=}1$ while other C4 models yield $\Kempirical{=}2$, we compute per-feature Cohen's $d$ (depth-1 vs.\ depth-2) and LightGBM feature importances across six C4 configurations spanning the full 1v2 AUC range (0.594--0.884).
638	
639	Mistral-7B exhibits the lowest depth-0 mean surprisal (2.32 vs.\ 2.90--3.02 for other models), the scorer model therefore already finds Mistral's output highly predictable.
640	This compressed baseline limits the dynamic range available for detecting inter-generational degradation: all 15 Cohen's $d$ values are weak ($\bar{|d|} = 0.112$; Table~\ref{tab:cohens_d_full}), and no single feature exceeds 13\% of total classifier importance.
641	The borderline models (LLaMA-8B, Pythia at $T{=}1.2$) show a similar pattern at slightly higher effect sizes.
642	
643	Greedy decoding is qualitatively distinct: Pythia-greedy achieves 0.884 AUC via lexical collapse (hapax ratio $d = -1.056$, capturing 47\% of importance), a signal absent under sampling-based generation where all non-greedy models rely on surprisal variability features.

both CI lower bounds $> \\theta$).\nThis convergence despite differences in effective LoRA adaptation capacity (Pythia uses fused QKV attention with rank-16 vs.\\ Qwen's separate Q/K/V projections) indicates robustness to LoRA parameterization.\nThe extended discriminability under retraining aligns with theoretical analyses of iterative retraining instability on synthetic data~\\cite{bertrand2024stability, gerstgrasser2024model}, where each retraining cycle amplifies distributional artifacts beyond the text-intrinsic degradation observed in prompt-chains.\nExtrapolating yields 7v8 $\\approx 0.57 < \\theta$ for both models. This places $\\Kempirical{=}7$ near the ceiling for retrain-chains.\nPrompt-chain $\\Kempirical$ represents the operationally relevant boundary for data curation (text-intrinsic information loss); retrain-chain $\\Kempirical$ represents the upper bound achievable via model archaeology (known training pipeline).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Retrain-chain pairwise $\\auc$ with 5-fold CV 95\\% CIs. Both models yield $\\Kempirical{=}7$; all CI lower bounds $> \\theta{=}0.60$. Retrain-chain CIs use fold-level estimates (Section~\\ref{sec:retrain_ci_note}).}\n\\label{tab:retrain_ci}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Qwen-1.5B}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Pythia-1.4B}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\n\\textbf{Pair} & $\\auc$ & 95\\% CI & $\\auc$ & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n0v1 & 0.9837 & $[.982,\\,.985]$ & 0.9844 & $[.983,\\,.986]$ \\\\\n1v2 & 0.8971 & $[.895,\\,.899]$ & 0.9470 & $[.942,\\,.952]$ \\\\\n2v3 & 0.8098 & $[.805,\\,.814]$ & 0.8891 & $[.882,\\,.896]$ \\\\\n3v4 & 0.7600 & $[.753,\\,.767]$ & 0.7999 & $[.795,\\,.805]$ \\\\\n4v5 & 0.7459 & $[.738,\\,.754]$ & 0.6907 & $[.688,\\,.693]$ \\\\\n5v6 & 0.7017 & $[.696,\\,.708]$ & 0.6497 & $[.643,\\,.657]$ \\\\\n6v7 & 0.6410 & $[.638,\\,.645]$ & 0.6340 & $[.628,\\,.641]$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{CI methodology note.}\n\\label{sec:retrain_ci_note}\nThe main pipeline reports bootstrap 95\\% CIs (1{,}000 or 10{,}000 resamples), which are valid because each bootstrap sample preserves the GroupKFold document structure: resampling operates on documents, and all depths of a given document remain together.\nIn the retrain-chain setting, each generation's training corpus is the \\emph{output} of the previous generation's fine-tuned model. This creates inter-generation dependencies that standard bootstrap resampling cannot preserve, and naive resampling would inflate AUC estimates.\nWe therefore report fold-based 95\\% CIs for retrain-chain experiments, computed as $\\bar{x} \\pm 1.96 \\cdot s / \\sqrt{K}$ from the $K{=}5$ GroupKFold AUC values.\n\n\\emph{Threshold sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:theta_sensitivity} reports $\\Kempirical$ across $\\theta \\in [0.55, 0.70]$ for representative configurations including arXiv and cross-scorer conditions.\nQwen-continuation's 1v2 CI lower bound exceeds $\\theta$ by only 0.2\\,pp; all ten boundary comparisons yield permutation $p < 0.001$.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{$\\Kempirical$ as a function of $\\theta$ across conditions. The \\textbf{domain ordering} (arXiv $>$ web $\\geq$ cross-scorer) and \\textbf{bounded range} ($\\Kempirical{\\leq}4$) are preserved across $\\theta \\in [0.55, 0.70]$. The headline result (web $\\Kempirical{=}2$, arXiv $\\Kempirical{=}3$) holds for $\\theta \\in [0.575, 0.625]$.}\n\\label{tab:theta_sensitivity}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Config} & $\\theta{=}0.55$ & $\\theta{=}0.60$ & $\\theta{=}0.625$ & $\\theta{=}0.70$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nC4 (web) & Qwen-rw      & 3 & 2 & 2 & 1 \\\\\nC4 (web) & Pythia       & 2 & 2 & 2 & 1 \\\\\nC4 (web) & OLMo         & 2 & 2 & 1 & 1 \\\\\nC4 (web) & Qwen-cont    & 3 & 2 & 2 & 1 \\\\\nC4 (web) & Qwen-7B      & 2 & 2 & 2 & 1 \\\\\nC4 (web) & Mistral-7B   & 1 & 1 & 1 & 1 \\\\\n\\midrule\narXiv    & Qwen-1.5B-base (cont.) & 4 & 3 & 3 & 2 \\\\\narXiv    & Pythia-1.4B (cont.)    & 4 & 3 & 3 & 2 \\\\\n\\midrule\nCross-scorer & Qwen-cont/Pythia-scored & 2 & 1 & 1 & 1 \\\\\nCross-scorer & Qwen-cont/OLMo-scored   & 2 & 1 & 1 & 1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_theta_sensitivity.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\Kempirical$ as a function of $\\theta$ across all model $\\times$ domain configurations (three panels: C4, arXiv, Wikipedia). The domain ordering (Wikipedia $\\Kempirical{\\leq}1$ $<$ C4 $\\Kempirical{\\leq}2$ $<$ arXiv $\\Kempirical{\\leq}3$) is preserved across the full $\\theta \\in [0.55, 0.70]$ range. Within each domain, inter-model variation is small relative to inter-domain differences. Source domain rather than model identity governs $\\Kempirical$.}\n\\label{fig:theta_sensitivity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\emph{Structural confound: token length.}\nIn continuation mode the generation prompt is discarded, so all depths $\\geq 1$ consist entirely of machine-generated tokens and the 1v2 boundary compares texts differing only in conditioning context (human-authored vs.\\ machine-generated prompt), not structural composition.\nDepth~0 texts average ${\\sim}$191 subword tokens (C4 truncation), while depth~1+ texts average 193--256 tokens depending on model (Table~\\ref{tab:token_length}).\nThis asymmetry is directionally opposite to a length-inflation confound: shorter depth-0 texts provide less feature-extraction context, making 0v1 discrimination harder, not easier.\nWithin depths 1--5, mean token counts are stable (within-model range $\\leq$ 4 tokens for all configurations except Qwen-continuation at 6 tokens), so the pairwise comparisons underlying $\\Kempirical{=}2$ are unaffected by length.\nEOS truncation rates are similarly depth-stable within each model: Qwen-rewrite 7--11\\%, Pythia 34--36\\%, OLMo $<$1\\%, Qwen-continuation 17--20\\%, Qwen-7B 45--47\\%.\nModels with the highest truncation (Pythia, Qwen-7B) still yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$ with the same step-function AUC decay pattern as low-truncation models (Table~\\ref{tab:kstar_ci}), EOS truncation therefore does not drive the boundary.\n\n\\emph{Graduated filtering.}\nAll $\\alpha$ values (0.1--0.9) underperform binary-ours on MMLU ($\\alpha{=}0.1$: 0.5873; $\\alpha{=}0.9$: 0.5892 vs.\\ 0.5941); at $\\alpha{=}0.9$, HellaSwag matches exactly (0.6701).\nBinary-ours significantly outperforms all alternatives on MMLU ($p_{\\text{corr}} \\leq 0.021$); Graduated$_{0.9}$ is indistinguishable on HellaSwag ($p = 1.00$) but worse on MMLU ($p_{\\text{corr}} = 0.011$).\n\\label{tab:pairwise_significance}\n\n\\subsection{Dose-Response: Contamination Ratio Effect}\n\\label{sec:dose_response}\n\nThe main filtering experiments (Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}) use 100\\% synthetic data to maximize effect size.\nTo characterize behavior at lower contamination ratios, we mix synthetic depths 1--5 with clean C4 at 10\\%, 30\\%, and 50\\% contamination (Qwen-1.5B continuation, 5 seeds each; Table~\\ref{tab:dose_response}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{MMLU and HellaSwag by contamination ratio and filtering strategy (5 seeds, mean $\\pm$ std). Base model MMLU: 0.6018. Binary filtering retains a significant advantage on HellaSwag across all ratios ($p \\leq 0.004$, Holm-Bonferroni corrected).}\n\\label{tab:dose_response}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{10\\%}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{30\\%}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{50\\%}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Strategy} & MMLU & HellaSwag & MMLU & HellaSwag & MMLU & HellaSwag \\\\\n\\midrule\nNo-filter   & $.6058{\\pm}.0002$ & $.6689{\\pm}.0006$ & $.6069{\\pm}.0007$ & $.6664{\\pm}.0018$ & $.6063{\\pm}.0012$ & $.6680{\\pm}.0005$ \\\\\nBinary-ours & $\\mathbf{.6079{\\pm}.0004}$ & $\\mathbf{.6727{\\pm}.0007}$ & $\\mathbf{.6082{\\pm}.0011}$ & $\\mathbf{.6725{\\pm}.0006}$ & $\\mathbf{.6088{\\pm}.0009}$ & $\\mathbf{.6731{\\pm}.0004}$ \\\\\nGraduated   & $.6066{\\pm}.0010$ & $.6700{\\pm}.0014$ & $.6072{\\pm}.0008$ & $.6665{\\pm}.0012$ & $.6062{\\pm}.0008$ & $.6679{\\pm}.0001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt all tested ratios (10--50\\%), no-filter MMLU remains above the base model (0.6018), synthetic data at these proportions is therefore net-beneficial rather than harmful.\nBinary filtering consistently improves over no-filter, with HellaSwag differences significant across all ratios ($p \\leq 0.004$, Holm-Bonferroni corrected).\nMMLU differences are smaller and significant only at 10\\% after correction ($p = 0.001$).\nGraduated weighting is indistinguishable from no-filter at all ratios (MMLU $p \\geq 0.27$; HellaSwag $p \\geq 0.06$), depth assignments beyond $\\Kempirical$ therefore inject noise regardless of contamination severity.\nEffect sizes diminish 3--6$\\times$ relative to the 100\\% setting (Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}), but the strategy ordering (binary $>$ graduated $\\approx$ no-filter) is preserved.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Filtering Replication}\n\\label{sec:pythia_filtering}\n\nTo test whether $\\Kempirical{=}2$ correctly predicts optimal curation granularity across architectures, we replicate the filtering experiment on Pythia-1.4B (LoRA rank 8, nucleus $p{=}0.9$, $T{=}1.0$, 3 seeds; Table~\\ref{tab:pythia_filtering}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pythia-1.4B filtering replication (3 seeds, mean $\\pm$ std). Strategy ordering matches Qwen (binary $>$ no-filter $>$ graduated on MMLU).}\n\\label{tab:pythia_filtering}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Strategy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{HellaSwag} \\\\\n\\midrule\nNo-filter & $.2568{\\pm}.0012$ & $.5250{\\pm}.0008$ \\\\\nBinary-ours & $\\mathbf{.2585{\\pm}.0004}$ & $.5251{\\pm}.0001$ \\\\\nGraduated$_{0.9}$ & $.2561{\\pm}.0009$ & $\\mathbf{.5253{\\pm}.0007}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe strategy ordering (binary $>$ no-filter $>$ graduated on MMLU) matches Qwen (Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}).\nThe small absolute differences reflect the model's near-chance MMLU baseline rather than filtering inefficacy; the consistent ordering across two architectures supports $\\Kempirical{=}2$'s predictive value for curation granularity.\nHellaSwag differences are negligible across all strategies.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Subset Stability}\n\\label{sec:feature_subset}\n\nTo test whether $\\Kempirical{=}2$ depends on the specific 15D feature combination, we evaluate random feature subsets of varying size on Pythia-1.4B prompt-chain data (Table~\\ref{tab:feature_subset}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{1v2 $\\auc$ by feature subset size (Pythia-1.4B). $\\Kempirical{=}2$ ($\\auc > \\theta{=}0.60$) is robust at $\\geq$8 features.}\n\\label{tab:feature_subset}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Subset} & \\textbf{Tested} & \\textbf{Pass} & \\textbf{Min $\\auc$} & \\textbf{Mean $\\auc$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n15D (full) & 1 & 1 & 0.6262 & 0.6262 \\\\\n10D        & 5 & 5 & 0.6153 & 0.6191 \\\\\n8D         & 5 & 5 & 0.6056 & 0.6159 \\\\\n5D         & 5 & 2 & 0.5856 & 0.5998 \\\\\n3D (top-3) & 1 & 0 & 0.5871 & 0.5871 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n$\\Kempirical{=}2$ holds for all subsets of size $\\geq$8 (10/10 pass, min $\\auc = 0.6056$), becomes fragile at 5D (2/5 pass), and fails at 3D (top-3 surprisal features alone: $\\auc = 0.5871 < \\theta$).\nThe boundary does not depend on any specific feature combination but requires sufficient dimensionality ($\\geq$8) to capture the multi-faceted distributional shift between generations 1 and 2.\n\n\\subsection{self\\_BLEU Feature Independence}\n\\label{sec:selfbleu_ablation}\n\nTo verify that $\\Kempirical$ does not depend on the self\\_BLEU feature (which may reflect stylistic repetition rather than depth-correlated degradation), we compare 15D and 14D (self\\_BLEU removed) classifiers across four model configurations (Table~\\ref{tab:selfbleu_ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{15D vs.\\ 14D (self\\_BLEU removed) 1v2 $\\auc$ with bootstrap 95\\% CIs. Removing self\\_BLEU does not change $\\Kempirical$ for any configuration. Maximum $|\\Delta\\auc| = 0.0031$, within bootstrap CI width (${\\sim}0.018$).}\n\\label{tab:selfbleu_ablation}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & $\\Kempirical$ & \\textbf{15D $\\auc$ [CI]} & \\textbf{14D $\\auc$ [CI]} & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-base   & 2/2 & $.6288$ $[.620,\\,.638]$ & $.6258$ $[.616,\\,.635]$ & $-.0031$ \\\\\nPythia-1.4B  & 2/2 & $.8840$ $[.878,\\,.890]$ & $.8830$ $[.876,\\,.889]$ & $-.0010$ \\\\\nLLaMA-8B     & 2/2 & $.6075$ $[.598,\\,.617]$ & $.6074$ $[.598,\\,.617]$ & $-.0001$ \\\\\nMistral-7B   & 1/1 & $.5902$ $[.580,\\,.600]$ & $.5903$ $[.580,\\,.600]$ & $+.0002$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll four configurations preserve their $\\Kempirical$ under 14D, with negligible AUC change ($|\\Delta| \\leq 0.003$).\nThe self\\_BLEU feature contributes minimally to the depth boundary; $\\Kempirical{=}2$ is driven by surprisal and entropy features rather than lexical repetition statistics.\n\n\\subsection{Wikipedia Domain: Scorer Confound Resolution}\n\\label{sec:wiki_domain}\n\nUnder same-family scoring (Qwen-1.5B scorer), Wikipedia $\\Kempirical$ appeared to exhibit model-family dependence: Qwen generators (1.5B and 7B) yielded $\\Kempirical{=}2$, while all four non-Qwen models yielded $\\Kempirical{=}1$ (Table~\\ref{tab:wiki_domain}).\nA scorer confound ablation resolves this apparent split.\nRescoring the same Qwen-7B Wikipedia text with Pythia-1.4B reduces 1v2 $\\auc$ from $0.611$ to $0.587$ ($\\Delta = -2.4$\\,pp), dropping $\\Kempirical$ from 2 to 1.\nWith the scorer confound controlled, all six models yield $\\Kempirical{=}1$ on Wikipedia, and the domain ordering simplifies to: arXiv ($\\Kempirical{=}3$) $>$ C4 ($\\Kempirical{=}2$) $>$ Wikipedia ($\\Kempirical{=}1$).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Wikipedia pairwise $\\auc$ across 6 models. $^\\dagger$Qwen rows use same-family scorer (Qwen-1.5B); cross-family scoring (Pythia-1.4B) reduces Qwen-7B to $\\Kempirical{=}1$ ($\\Delta = -2.4$\\,pp), resolving the apparent family split.}\n\\label{tab:wiki_domain}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{0v1} & \\textbf{1v2} & \\textbf{2v3} & $\\Kempirical$ & \\textbf{Scorer} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B (base) & Qwen & ---  & .619 & .550 & 2$^\\dagger$ & Qwen-1.5B \\\\\nQwen-1.5B (base) & Qwen & ---  & .599 & ---  & 1 & Pythia-1.4B \\\\\nQwen-7B          & Qwen & .967 & .611 & .556 & 2$^\\dagger$ & Qwen-1.5B \\\\\nQwen-7B          & Qwen & ---  & .587 & ---  & 1 & Pythia-1.4B \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B   & non-Qwen & .975 & .585 & .545 & 1 & Qwen-1.5B \\\\\nOLMo-1B       & non-Qwen & .993 & .499 & .501 & 1 & Qwen-1.5B \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B  & non-Qwen & .932 & .595 & .539 & 1 & Qwen-1.5B \\\\\nMistral-7B    & non-Qwen & .961 & .555 & .524 & 1 & Qwen-1.5B \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$Same-family scorer; cross-family scoring yields $\\Kempirical{=}1$.}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-fold transparency.}\nFor Qwen-1.5B, cross-family scoring (Pythia-1.4B) yields $\\auc$(1v2) $= 0.5994$, with per-fold values $[0.596, 0.606, 0.600, 0.603, 0.592]$ (2/5 folds marginally above $\\theta = 0.60$).\nThe aggregate mean falls below $\\theta$, confirming $\\Kempirical{=}1$.\nFor Qwen-7B, the corresponding $\\auc$ drops further to $0.587$.\n\n\\subsection{ArXiv Cross-Scorer Bootstrap CIs}\n\\label{sec:arxiv_crossscorer_ci}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Bootstrap 95\\% CI for arXiv cross-scorer (Pythia-1.4B) 2v3 boundary $\\auc$ (10{,}000 resamples). Point estimates classify 3/4 generators as $\\Kempirical{=}3$; strict CI lower-bound criterion confirms 2/4.}\n\\label{tab:arxiv_crossscorer_ci}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Generator} & \\textbf{2v3 $\\auc$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{CI lower $> \\theta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B    & 0.632 & $[0.617,\\,0.646]$ & Yes \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & 0.620 & $[0.605,\\,0.636]$ & Yes (0.5\\,pp margin) \\\\\nMistral-7B   & 0.608 & $[0.593,\\,0.622]$ & No \\\\\nQwen-7B      & 0.599 & $[0.584,\\,0.613]$ & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{JSD Decay Analysis}\n\\label{sec:jsd_extended}\n\nThe token-level JSD between adjacent generations reveals two distinct decay regimes (Figure~\\ref{fig:jsd_decay}).\nFor C4 web text, 0v1 JSD exceeds 1v2 by 6--56$\\times$ across all 8 models (1B--14B) with $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (median cliff ratio ${\\sim}$15$\\times$), and subsequent pairs converge to a noise floor of ${\\sim}$0.003--0.005.\nFitting $\\jsd(k, k{+}1) \\propto e^{-\\lambda k}$ yields $\\lambda \\approx 2.2$--$3.5$ for C4 (mean ${\\approx}\\,2.9$), consistent with rapid mixing of $G_\\delta$ toward its stationary distribution.\nThese decay rates represent qualitative characterizations from 5-point curves rather than precise estimates.\nThe empirical decay rate $\\lambda$ is consistent within each domain but varies across domains: C4 yields $\\lambda \\approx 2.9$, Wikipedia $\\lambda \\approx 3.1$ (excluding OLMo, whose $\\lambda = 6.3$ reflects a capacity-limited collapse: 0v1 $\\jsd = 0.169$ with near-complete information loss at the first step, the exponential fit is therefore unreliable), and arXiv $\\lambda \\approx 1.0$.\nThis cross-model consistency indicates that the contraction rate of $G_\\delta$ is governed primarily by the structure of the input domain rather than the model architecture.\nArXiv text decays ${\\sim}$3$\\times$ slower than C4 ($\\lambda \\approx 1.0$ vs.\\ $2.9$), with a correspondingly higher convergence floor (${\\sim}$0.007--0.009 vs.\\ ${\\sim}$0.003--0.004).\nFormulaic structural features (LaTeX formatting, citation patterns, section templates) degrade more slowly under autoregressive generation and supply persistent depth-distinguishing signal, this accounts for the elevated $\\Kempirical{=}3$.\nThe retrain-chain exhibits a fundamentally different profile: $\\lambda = 0.285$ with a cliff ratio of only 1.31$\\times$ and no sharp drop between adjacent pairs (Figure~\\ref{fig:retrain_decay}).\nThis gradual, sustained divergence reflects cumulative model-adaptation artifacts rather than text-intrinsic information loss, further supporting the distinction between prompt-chain and retrain-chain $\\Kempirical$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:boundary_extended}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_retrain_decay.png}\n\\caption{Retrain-chain AUC decay across 7 generation pairs (Qwen2.5-1.5B LoRA). Unlike prompt-chain (sharp cliff at 0v1$\\to$1v2), retrain-chain AUC decays gradually from 0.984 to 0.641, with all pairs above $\\theta{=}0.60$. Error bars: 95\\% CI from 5-fold CV. Dashed line: $\\theta{=}0.60$; extrapolated 7v8 $\\approx 0.57$ (below $\\theta$), suggesting $\\Kempirical{=}7$ is near the ceiling.}\n\\label{fig:retrain_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Boundary Cases and Scale Effect}\n\\label{sec:boundary_extended}\n\nOf 9 tested model configurations on C4, 8 yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$; Mistral-7B is the only sub-2 exception ($\\Kempirical{=}1$; 1v2 $\\auc = 0.594$, CI $[0.584, 0.603]$), validating $\\theta{=}0.60$ as an effective discriminator (gap: $0.594$ vs.\\ $0.611$--$0.696$).\nMistral's cross-domain profile reinforces the domain-governed interpretation: despite $\\Kempirical{=}1$ on C4 and Wikipedia, it yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$ on code and $\\Kempirical{=}3$ on arXiv (1v2 $\\auc = 0.820$, 2v3 $= 0.628$), and this profile persists under cross-scorer validation (Pythia-1.4B scorer: C4 $\\Kempirical{=}1$, arXiv $\\Kempirical{=}3$ (2v3 $\\auc = 0.608$, CI $[0.593, 0.622]$)), domain lexical diversity can therefore rescue even web-text one-wash generators (Table~\\ref{tab:domain_kstar}).\nLarger models produce more human-like text, reducing 0v1 JSD: 0.16--0.29 at $\\leq$1.5B, 0.077--0.13 at 7--14B.\nHowever, Qwen-14B still yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\\auc = 0.621$, CI $[0.612, 0.630]$), with 0v1 $\\auc = 0.960$ comparable to Qwen-7B ($0.964$), scale in the 1B--14B range therefore does not shift the discrimination boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Sample-size control.}\nAs a sample-size control, subsampling C4 to $n{=}2{,}200$ (matching arXiv) yields $\\Kempirical{=}1$ (1v2 $\\auc = 0.601$, CI $[0.586, 0.616]$).\nReduced sample size widens confidence intervals and produces more conservative $\\Kempirical$ estimates, arXiv's $\\Kempirical{=}3$ at the same sample size therefore reflects genuine domain signal rather than a statistical artifact of smaller training sets.\n\n\\subsection{LaTeX Markup Ablation (arXiv)}\n\\label{sec:latex_ablation}\n\nTo disentangle content-level depth signal from formatting artifacts on arXiv, we strip all LaTeX markup (commands, environments, inline math delimiters) and re-extract features from the plain-text residual (Qwen-1.5B continuation, $n{=}2{,}200$; Table~\\ref{tab:latex_ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{arXiv pairwise $\\auc$ with and without LaTeX markup (Qwen-1.5B continuation). Removing formatting reduces 2v3 by ${\\sim}$1.8\\,pp but $\\Kempirical$ remains at 3 (CI lower $> \\theta$). LaTeX stripping fraction increases with depth (0.6\\% at depth 0, 4.6\\% at depth 5), deeper generations therefore produce proportionally more formatting tokens.}\n\\label{tab:latex_ablation}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Pair} & \\textbf{Original} & \\textbf{Stripped} & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0v1 & 0.9946 & 0.9948 & $+$0.0002 \\\\\n1v2 & 0.8580 & 0.8297 & $-$0.028 \\\\\n2v3 & 0.6427 $[0.628,\\,0.658]$ & 0.6248 $[0.610,\\,0.640]$ & $-$0.018 \\\\\n3v4 & 0.5437 & 0.5468 & $+$0.003 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\Kempirical$ & 3 & 3 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nContent-level statistical properties (surprisal distribution, vocabulary diversity) account for the large majority of the depth signal; LaTeX formatting contributes a modest but measurable increment (${\\sim}$1.8\\,pp at the boundary pair).\nThe depth-correlated increase in LaTeX stripping fraction (depth 0: $-$0.6\\%, depth 5: $-$4.6\\%) indicates that autoregressive generation progressively introduces formatting tokens, consistent with mode-seeking behavior in structured text.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Power Analysis}\n\\label{sec:power_curve}\n\nTo characterize the minimum sample size required for reliable $\\Kempirical$ determination, we subsample C4 at $n \\in \\{500, 1000, 2000, 3000, 4000, 5000\\}$ and measure 1v2 AUC stability across 10 resamples per size (Qwen-1.5B-base continuation, 5-fold GroupKFold; Table~\\ref{tab:power_curve}, Figure~\\ref{fig:power_curve}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Subsampling power analysis for $\\Kempirical{=}2$ determination (C4, Qwen-1.5B-base, 10 resamples per $n$). Detection probability: fraction of resamples where 1v2 $\\auc > \\theta{=}0.60$.}\n\\label{tab:power_curve}\n\\begin{tabular}{rccc}\n\\toprule\n$n$ & \\textbf{Mean 1v2 $\\auc$} & \\textbf{Std} & \\textbf{Detection Prob.} \\\\\n\\midrule\n500 & 0.581 & 0.018 & 20\\% \\\\\n1{,}000 & 0.591 & 0.014 & 30\\% \\\\\n2{,}000 & 0.610 & 0.009 & 100\\% \\\\\n3{,}000 & 0.617 & 0.005 & 100\\% \\\\\n4{,}000 & 0.622 & 0.004 & 100\\% \\\\\n5{,}000 & 0.629 & 0.000 & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_power_curve.png}\n\\caption{$\\Kempirical{=}2$ detection rate (left axis, blue) and mean 1v2 $\\auc$ (right axis, green) as a function of sample size $n$. Detection rate reaches 100\\% at $n{=}2{,}000$; the 80\\% power threshold (dotted purple) is crossed between $n{=}1{,}000$ and $n{=}2{,}000$. Error bars: $\\pm$1 std over 10 resamples. The dashed red line marks $\\theta{=}0.60$.}\n\\label{fig:power_curve}\n\\end{figure}\n\nDetection rate jumps from 20\\% ($n{=}500$) to 100\\% ($n{=}2{,}000$), with the 80\\% power threshold crossed near $n{=}1{,}500$.\nAt $n{=}5{,}000$ (the sample size used in all main experiments), variance is zero across resamples, our results are therefore well above the minimum required for reliable boundary estimation.\nThe sharp transition reflects the proximity of the 1v2 AUC to $\\theta$: small increases in $n$ reduce variance sufficiently to move all resamples above the threshold.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Group Ablation}\n\\label{sec:feature_ablation}\n\nTo assess the relative contribution of each feature group to depth discrimination, we train a plain LightGBM classifier (\\texttt{n\\_estimators}$=$200, \\texttt{max\\_depth}$=$6; distinct from the OBD-LightGBM used in Section~\\ref{sec:method}) on the 1\\,vs.\\,2 task using (a) each feature group alone (\\emph{single}) and (b) the full 15D set minus one group (\\emph{leave-one-out}).\nRepresentative models per domain: LLaMA-3.1-8B (C4), Qwen-1.5B continuation (arXiv), and the domain ablation configuration (Wikipedia).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_feature_ablation.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature group ablation heatmap for 1\\,vs.\\,2 classification AUC. \\emph{Left}: single-group performance. \\emph{Right}: leave-one-out performance. Surprisal (base) features are the dominant signal on arXiv (single AUC $= 0.809$) but provide only weak marginal signal on C4 and Wikipedia.}\n\\label{fig:feature_ablation}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Feature group ablation for 1\\,vs.\\,2 classification (AUC). \\textbf{Single}: using only features from one group. \\textbf{Leave-one-out}: removing one group from the full 15D feature set. $\\Delta$ denotes the AUC drop from the full model. Classifier: plain LightGBM (\\texttt{n\\_estimators}$=$200, \\texttt{max\\_depth}$=$6), distinct from the OBD-LightGBM pipeline; AUC values are not directly comparable to Table~\\ref{tab:kstar_ci}.}\n\\label{tab:feature_ablation}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Single Group}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Leave-One-Out ($\\Delta$)}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\nFeature Group & C4 & arXiv & Wiki & C4 & arXiv & Wiki \\\\\n\\midrule\nSurprisal (base) & 0.597 & 0.809 & 0.581 & 0.601 ($-$0.007) & 0.828 ($-$0.031) & 0.616 ($-$0.003) \\\\\nSurprisal (deriv.) & 0.590 & 0.789 & 0.590 & 0.612 (+0.003) & 0.856 ($-$0.003) & 0.623 (+0.004) \\\\\nLexical diversity & 0.550 & 0.691 & 0.557 & 0.608 ($-$0.001) & 0.855 ($-$0.005) & 0.614 ($-$0.005) \\\\\nFreq. distribution & 0.533 & 0.684 & 0.564 & 0.604 ($-$0.005) & 0.851 ($-$0.009) & 0.612 ($-$0.007) \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull (15D) & 0.609 & 0.860 & 0.619 & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{---} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nSurprisal (base) features dominate on arXiv (single-group AUC $= 0.809$; LOO drop $= 0.031$, the largest across all domain--group combinations), consistent with the persistent surprisal-distribution signal in structured academic text.\nOn C4 and Wikipedia, all LOO drops are small ($\\leq 0.007$, within cross-fold standard deviation), no single feature group is therefore individually critical for the weaker low-$\\Kempirical$ signal.\nRemoving surprisal derivatives increases AUC on C4 (+0.003) and Wikipedia (+0.004). This indicates partial redundancy with the base surprisal group in low-$\\Kempirical$ domains.\n\n\\subsection{Classifier Choice: McNemar Test}\n\\label{sec:mcnemar_baselines}\n\nTo verify that depth discriminability is driven by the 15D feature representation rather than classifier choice, we compare OBD-LightGBM against three baseline classifiers (logistic regression, linear SVC, random forest) on the depth-1 vs.\\ depth-2 binary classification task using McNemar's test with continuity correction on 5-fold GroupKFold out-of-fold predictions (Table~\\ref{tab:mcnemar_obd_baselines}).\nOf 36 comparisons, 31 are non-significant ($p \\geq 0.05$), and all five significant results concentrate in two model $\\times$ domain conditions.\nFor LLaMA-8B on C4, all three baselines significantly outperform OBD ($p \\leq 0.001$), likely reflecting LightGBM overfitting on the relatively noisy 8B-generated features rather than a systematic classifier advantage.\nFor Qwen-1.5B on Wikipedia, OBD outperforms both linear classifiers ($p < 0.001$) but not random forest ($p = 0.655$).\n\n\\begin{table*}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{McNemar test $p$-values comparing OBD-LightGBM against baseline classifiers on the depth-1 vs.\\ depth-2 binary classification task (5-fold GroupKFold, out-of-fold predictions). Classifier accuracy shown in parentheses. Significant results ($p < 0.05$) are \\textbf{bold}; $^{*}p<0.05$, $^{**}p<0.01$, $^{***}p<0.001$.}\n\\label{tab:mcnemar_obd_baselines}\n\\resizebox{\\textwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{OBD Acc.} & \\textbf{vs.\\ 5/36 reach $p < 0.05$; 4/36 reach $p < 0.01$.\nThe predominance of non-significant results confirms that depth discriminability is feature-driven, not classifier-driven.\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Domain Lexical Statistics}\n\\label{sec:domain_lexical_stats}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:domain-ttr-entropy} reports type-token ratio (TTR) and vocabulary entropy across three domains and six depth levels.\nThe TTR ordering at depth 0 (arXiv $>$ C4 $>$ Wikipedia) aligns with the $\\Kempirical$ ordering, with large effect sizes (Cohen's $d = 1.52$ for arXiv vs.\\ Wikipedia).\nVocabulary entropy shows a partial reversal at depth 0: C4 ($H = 6.225$) falls below Wikipedia ($H = 6.408$), while arXiv remains highest ($H = 6.853$).\nThis reversal likely reflects C4's greater content heterogeneity (higher variance: $\\sigma = 0.706$ vs.\\ $0.284$), which inflates TTR but depresses mean entropy.\nAs depth increases, all domains converge, with arXiv exhibiting the steepest decline, consistent with its higher $\\Kempirical$ and slower convergence to the stationary distribution.\n\n\\begin{table*}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Type-Token Threshold Validation}\n\\label{sec:theta_lofo}\n\nTo validate $\\theta{=}0.60$ without circular reasoning, we perform leave-one-family-out (LOFO) cross-validation: each fold removes one architecture family, derives $\\hat{\\theta}$ as the midpoint between $\\min(\\auc_{1v2})$ and $\\max(\\auc_{2v3})$ over the remaining families, then predicts $\\Kempirical$ for the held-out models.\nTable~\\ref{tab:theta_lofo} reports results across all three domains.\n\nOn C4, 8/9 held-out predictions agree with the original $\\Kempirical$ assignment at $\\theta{=}0.60$ (agreement: 89\\%).\nThe single mismatch is Qwen-1.5B-RW (rewrite mode), whose 2v3 AUC of 0.5893 lies above the LOFO-derived $\\hat{\\theta}{=}0.5725$ but below the global $\\theta{=}0.60$; this configuration is also the only rewrite-mode model, the discrepancy is therefore attributable to mode rather than threshold instability.\nThe derived $\\hat{\\theta}$ values span $[0.5725, 0.6004]$, and $\\theta{=}0.60$ falls within this range.\n\nArXiv corroborates with 6/6 agreement (100\\%), though the wide $\\hat{\\theta}$ range $[0.5735, 0.7296]$ reflects OLMo-1B's near-chance performance ($\\auc_{1v2}{=}0.503$), which inflates the gap midpoint when OLMo is included in the training fold.\n\nWikipedia yields only 1/6 agreement (17\\%).\nThis failure is expected: most models produce near-chance 1v2 AUC on Wikipedia ($0.499$--$0.619$), so the gap between $\\min(\\auc_{1v2})$ and $\\max(\\auc_{2v3})$ is narrow or inverted, the gap-based threshold derivation is therefore invalid.\nWikipedia's $\\Kempirical{=}1$ assignment is independently confirmed by the global $\\theta{=}0.60$ criterion (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_domain}); LOFO simply cannot validate this because the method requires a clear AUC discontinuity between boundary and non-boundary pairs.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO) cross-validation of $\\theta$ across three domains. C4 (primary): 8/9 agreement (89\\%). arXiv: 6/6 (100\\%). Wikipedia: 1/6 (17\\%, see text).}\n\\label{tab:theta_lofo}\n\\begin{tabular}{l l l c c c c c c}\n\\toprule\nDomain & Held-out & Model & $\\text{AUC}_{1\\text{v}2}$ & $\\text{AUC}_{2\\text{v}3}$ & (Eq.~\\ref{eq:kstar}).}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-scorer validation (Code domain).}\nTo rule out scorer confound for Code $\\Kempirical{=}2$, we re-evaluate Qwen2.5-1.5B code generations using Pythia-1.4B as the reference scorer (Table~\\ref{tab:code_crossscorer}).\nCross-family scoring yields $\\Kempirical{=}2$ (1v2 $\\auc = 0.7197$, five-fold minimum $= 0.6985$, all folds above $\\theta$), a decrease of only 1.9\\,pp from Qwen self-scoring ($0.7382$).\nThis contrasts with Wikipedia, where same-family scoring inflates $\\Kempirical$ from 1 to 2 (Appendix~\\ref{sec:wiki_domain}): Code depth signal is robust to scorer choice, while Wikipedia's marginal signal is scorer-sensitive.\nThe scorer confound is therefore domain-specific rather than systematic.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Code domain cross-scorer validation: Pythia-1.4B scorer on Qwen2.5-1.5B code generations. $\\Kempirical{=}2$ is preserved; five-fold 0.7382 & 0.7093 \\\\\nPythia-1.4B (cross) & 0.7197 & 0.6985 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\Delta$ & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$-1.9$\\,pp} \\\\\n$\\Kempirical$ & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{2 (both)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Borderline Feature Analysis}\n\\label{sec:borderline_analysis}\n\nTo understand why Mistral-7B yields $\\Kempirical{=}1$ while other C4 models yield $\\Kempirical{=}2$, we compute per-feature Cohen's $d$ (depth-1 vs.\\ depth-2) and LightGBM feature importances across six C4 configurations spanning the full 1v2 AUC range (0.594--0.884).\n\nMistral-7B exhibits the lowest depth-0 mean surprisal (2.32 vs.\\ 2.90--3.02 for other models), the scorer model therefore already finds Mistral's output highly predictable.\nThis compressed baseline limits the dynamic range available for detecting inter-generational degradation: all 15 Cohen's $d$ values are weak ($\\bar{|d|} = 0.112$; Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d_full}), and no single feature exceeds 13\\% of total classifier importance.\nThe borderline models (LLaMA-8B, Pythia at $T{=}1.2$) show a similar pattern at slightly higher effect sizes.\n\nGreedy decoding is qualitatively distinct: Pythia-greedy achieves 0.884 AUC via lexical collapse (hapax ratio $d = -1.056$, capturing 47\\% of importance), a signal absent under sampling-based generation where all non-greedy models rely on surprisal variability features.\n\n\\begin{table*}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Feature-level summary of C4 1v2 classification across model categories. ```text
I now have a comprehensive understanding of the paper. Let me also check the project config for external review settings.

Let me now summarize what I know and proceed with the review:

**Paper Summary:**
- Title: "Generational Depth Estimation of Recursive Synthetic Text: Measuring the Discrimination Boundary"
- Target: EMNLP 2026 (ARR May 2026 cycle)
- Deadline: May 25, 2026 (6 days away)
- Format: ACL `[review]` template, long paper

**Key Findings:**
1. Measures K* (maximum discriminable generational depth) across 9 model configs (5 architecture families, 1B-14B), 4 domains, 4 decoding strategies


2. Results show K*=2 for web text across most configs, K*=1-2 for code, K*=3 for arXiv, and K*=1 for Wikipedia, with a "one-wash laundering" effect observed in web text
3. Generator-independent features achieve 80-92% transfer retention, and binary filtering outperforms graduated filtering in line with the K*=2 pattern

**Existing Issues:**
- All prior issues are resolved except:
  - R8-W4 (in_progress): Transfer matrix only covers 4/9 configs (≤1.5B), 7B cross-model transfer experiment in progress
  - R10-N1 (in_progress): McNemar claim evidence chain - action items done but resolution not yet confirmed

**EMNLP 2026 Guidelines:**
- ARR submission deadline: May 25, 2026
- Long paper: 8-page limit for main text, unlimited references
- Limitations and Ethics don't count toward page limit
- Double-blind review, no anonymity period, ACL template

Now I need to check the project config for external review settings, decide on reviewer arrangement, and dispatch visual check, external review, and internal reviewers. For EMNLP 2026, I'll assign 2 reviewers—one focused on method rigor and experimental validity, the other on novelty, completeness, and presentation.

Let me pull up the project config first.
```
